    Headline
    anxiety, depression, emotional lability, mania, paranoia, psychosis, suicidal ideation
    General Information
    Serious psychiatric adverse events have been reported with the use of efavirenz. Commonly reported psychiatric symptoms during efavirenz therapy include depression (up to 19%; severe 2.4%), anxiety (up to 13%), and nervousness (up to 7%). In controlled trials (including 1,008 efavirenz-treated patients and 635 patients treated with control regimens), the frequency of specific serious psychiatric events among patients who received efavirenz included suicidal ideation (0.7%), nonfatal suicide attempts (0.5%), aggressive behavior (0.4%), paranoid reactions (paranoia or psychosis) (0.4%), and manic reactions (mania) (0.2%). There have also been occasional postmarketing reports of aggressive reactions, catatonia, death by suicide, delusions, emotional lability, neurosis and psychosis-like behavior.

    Headline
    erythema, erythema multiforme, maculopapular rash, photosensitivity, pruritus, rash, Stevens-Johnson syndrome, urticaria
    General Information
    Skin rash occurred in 26% of adult patients and 32% of pediatric patients who received efavirenz during clinical trials. Efavirenz associated skin rashes are usually mild-to-moderate maculopapular rashes that occur within the first 2 weeks of initiating therapy. In most cases, the rash resolves within 1 month while continuing therapy. Compared with adults, children tend to have more frequent and severe rashes. During clinical trials, Grade 3 rash (confluent rash with pyrexia, generalized rash) occurred in 0.8% of adults and 1.1% of children. Grade 4 rash (erythema multiforme, Stevens-Johnson syndrome) occurred in 0.1% of the adults and 2.2% of children. Other dermatologic events reported in 5% to 16% of adults during clinical trials included allergic rash, erythema, erythematous rash, follicular rash, hives, inflammation, maculopapular rash, petechial rash, pruritus, pustular rash, urticaria, and welts. The discontinuation rate for rash was 1.7% for adults and 2.7% of pediatric patients in trials. During postmarketing experience, erythema multiforme, photosensitivity (photoallergic dermatitis), and Steven's Johnson syndrome have been reported. Discontinue the use of efavirenz in patients with a rash associated with blistering, desquamation (e.g., exfoliative dermatitis), mucosal involvement, or fever. The drug may be restarted following interruptions due to rash; however, for those patients experiencing a life-threatening cutaneous reaction (Stevens-Johnson syndrome), an alternative treatment is recommended. The use of appropriate antihistamines and corticosteroids may be considered when efavirenz is restarted; these agents may improve the tolerability and hasten the resolution of the rash. Consider prophylaxis with antihistamines prior to initiating therapy with efavirenz in pediatric patients.

    Headline
    elevated hepatic enzymes, hepatic failure, hepatitis
    General Information
    Elevated hepatic enzymes have been observed in patients treated with efavirenz. Among patients treated with 600 mg of efavirenz in controlled clinical trials, 2% to 8% developed AST and ALT levels greater than 5-times the upper limit of normal (ULN). In patients treated with efavirenz who were seropositive for hepatitis B or C, 13% developed AST and 20% developed ALT levels greater than 5-times the ULN. Elevations of gamma-glutamyltransferase (GGT) to 5-times the ULN were observed in up to 8% of patients. Isolated elevation of GGT in patients receiving efavirenz may reflect enzyme induction not associated with liver toxicity. There have been postmarketing reports of hepatitis and hepatic failure, requiring transplantation or resulting in death, in patients with no pre-existing hepatic disease or other identifiable risk factors. Monitoring of liver enzymes prior to and during treatment with efavirenz is recommended in all patients. Consider treatment discontinuation in patients with persistently elevated hepatic enzymes of greater than 5-times ULN. Discontinue efavirenz if elevation of hepatic enzymes is accompanied by clinical signs or symptoms of hepatitis or hepatic decompensation.

    Headline
    hypercholesterolemia, hyperlipidemia, hypertriglyceridemia
    General Information
    Increases in total cholesterol of 10% to 20% have been observed in healthy volunteers receiving efavirenz. In patients treated with efavirenz in combination with other antiretrovirals, increases in non-fasting total cholesterol and HDL of 20% to 40% and 25% to 35%, respectively, were observed. Non-fasting total cholesterol concentrations of 240 mg/dL or more and 300 mg/dL or more were observed in 28% to 54% and 4% to 20% of efavirenz recipients, respectively. Increases in triglycerides to greater than 750 mg/dL have been observed in 6% to 11% of efavirenz recipients. The significance of hypercholesterolemia and hypertriglyceridemia in patients treated with efavirenz is unknown. If a patient develops hyperlipidemia during antiretroviral treatment, possible interventions include dietary modification, use of lipid lowering agents, or modification of the treatment regimen. Monitoring of serum cholesterol and triglycerides is recommended during efavirenz therapy.

    hyperamylasemia, pancreatitis

The clinical significance of asymptomatic increases in serum amylase is unknown. Pancreatitis has been reported, although a causal relationship with efavirenz has not been established.

    Headline
    breast enlargement, Cushingoid features, gynecomastia, lipodystrophy
    General Information
    While more commonly associated with protease inhibitor therapy, a lipodystrophy syndrome consisting of redistribution or accumulation of body fat including central obesity, dorsocervical fat enlargement (buffalo hump), peripheral wasting, accumulation of facial fat, lipomas, breast enlargement, gynecomastia, and other Cushingoid features has been reported in patients receiving long-term highly active antiretroviral therapy (HAART) that includes efavirenz. The mechanism by which nucleoside analogues may cause body fat changes is not known. It has been suggested that nucleoside analogs may damage the mitochondria of adipocytes. An increased incidence of body fat changes is noted in those patients receiving long-term nucleoside therapy and in female patients.

    Headline
    teratogenesis
    General Information
    According to the manufacturer, retrospective case reports have described cases of teratogenesis (i.e., neural tube defects) in infants whose mothers were exposed to efavirenz-containing regimens during the first trimester of pregnancy. However, data from a meta-analysis of 23 studies found no increased risk of overall birth defects in infants born to mothers on efavirenz during the first trimester compared with those on other antiretroviral medications (relative risk 0.78; 95% CI, 0.56 to 1.08). Further, data from more than 7,900 periconception exposures to efavirenz in Botswana were sufficient to rule out an increased risk of neural tube defects with efavirenz.

    Headline

    Headline
    osteopenia, osteoporosis, vitamin D deficiency
    General Information
    Efavirenz has been associated with vitamin D deficiency and low bone mineral density, which may increase the risk for osteoporosis and osteopenia. To minimize the risk of osteopenia and osteoporosis development, monitor calcium and vitamin D concentrations in all patients receiving an antiretroviral regimen containing efavirenz. Initiate calcium and vitamin D supplementation for those with calcium or vitamin D deficiency.

    Headline
    autoimmune disease, Guillain-Barre syndrome, immune reconstitution syndrome
    General Information
    Immune reconstitution syndrome has been reported in patients treated with combination antiretroviral therapy. During the initial phase of HIV treatment, patients whose immune system responds to efavirenz therapy may develop an inflammatory response to indolent or residual opportunistic infections (such as progressive multifocal leukoencephalopathy (PML), Mycobacterium avium complex (MAC), cytomegalovirus (CMV), Pneumocystis pneumonia (PCP), or tuberculosis (TB)), which may necessitate further evaluation and treatment. In addition, autoimmune disease (including Graves' disease, Guillain-Barre syndrome, autoimmune hepatitis, and polymyositis) may develop; the time to onset is variable and may occur months after treatment initiation.

    Headline
    laboratory test interference
    General Information
    Efavirenz does not bind to cannabinoid receptors. However, a laboratory test interference has been reported with some screening assays that resulted in false-positive urine cannabinoid test results in patients receiving efavirenz. Confirmation of positive screening tests for cannabinoids by a more specific assay is recommended.

Description
  Text
  Efavirenz is a first-generation non-nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitor (NNRTI) approved for use in combination with other antiretroviral agents to treat human immunodeficiency virus type 1 (HIV-1) infections in adults and pediatric patients aged at least 3 months and weighing at least 3.5 kg. Although once recommended as a first-line treatment option for HIV-1, guidelines no longer recommend use of efavirenz due to the emergence of integrase strand transfer inhibitors (INSTIs), its low barrier to resistance, potential for drug interactions, and its tolerance profile. Additionally, similar to other NNRTIs, efavirenz is not recommended to treat HIV-2 infections, as HIV-2 is intrinsically resistant to NNRTIs. Notable adverse reactions include central nervous system symptoms (typically beginning within the first two days of therapy and generally resolve after 2 to 4 weeks), psychiatric experiences (including severe depression, suicidal ideation, and aggressive behavior), and QT prolongation. Skin rash is another common adverse reaction, particularly in pediatric patients, and can range from mild to severe, with rare cases of Stevens-Johnson syndrome. Efavirenz has also been linked to cases of hepatitis and liver failure and is not recommended for patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment. Efavirenz was approved by the FDA in 1998.

Mechanism Of Action
  Text
  Efavirenz is an HIV non-nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitor (NNRTI). It binds directly and noncompetitively to a site on reverse transcriptase that is distinct from where nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitors (NRTIs) bind. This binding induces a conformational change in the enzyme, disrupting its catalytic site and thereby inhibiting DNA polymerase activities. Unlike NRTIs, efavirenz does not compete with template or nucleoside triphosphates for incorporation into viral DNA and does not require phosphorylation to be active. By inhibiting reverse transcriptase, efavirenz interferes with the generation of DNA copies from viral RNA, which are essential for the synthesis of new virions.
 
Pharmacodynamics
Efavirenz is virustatic rather than virucidal, meaning it suppresses viral replication but does not eliminate the virus from the body. Efavirenz exhibits antiviral activity against various HIV-1 subtypes; the 90% to 95% inhibitory concentrations (EC90 to 95) range from 1.7 to 25 nM. Avoid use of efavirenz in patients with HIV-2 infection, as HIV-2 is intrinsically resistant to NNRTIs. To identify the HIV strain, The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention guidelines for HIV diagnostic testing recommend initial HIV testing using an HIV-1 and HIV-2 antigen and antibody combination immunoassay and subsequent testing using an HIV-1 and HIV-2 antibody differentiation immunoassay.
 
Resistance
Resistance to efavirenz can develop rapidly, particularly when it is administered as part of an inadequately suppressive treatment regimen. The emergence of resistance is associated with specific mutations in the reverse transcriptase enzyme. In efavirenz resistant patients, mutations in reverse transcriptase occurred most frequently at amino acid position 103. Other reverse transcriptase mutations occurring at 1 or more amino acid positions 98, 100, 101, 106, 108, 188, 190, 225, and 227 are also associated with resistance to efavirenz. Of these failure isolates, 61% had an 88-fold (median) decrease in cell culture efavirenz susceptibility. Cross-resistance to NNRTIs has been observed. HIV-1 strains previously characterized as efavirenz-resistant were also phenotypically resistant to nevirapine and delavirdine.

Pharmacokinetics
  Route Specific Pharmacokinetics
    Name
    Oral Route
    Description
    Peak efavirenz plasma concentrations were observed 3 to 5 hours after administration of doses of 200, 400, and 600 mg to patients with HIV. Steady-state plasma concentrations in these patients were reached in 6 to 10 days. For the tablets, a high-fat/high-caloric meal (e.g., 1,000 kcal, 50% to 60% calories from fat) increased the AUC by 28% and the maximum plasma concentration (Cmax) by 79% compared to fasting conditions. For the capsules, a high-fat/high-caloric meal (e.g., 894 kcal, 54% of total calories from fat) increased the AUC and Cmax by 22% and 39%, respectively; a reduced-fat/normal-caloric meal (e.g., 440 kcal, 4% calories from fat) increased the AUC and Cmax by 17% and 51%, respectively, as compared to fasting conditions. Administration of the capsule contents with 2 teaspoons of applesauce, grape jelly, yogurt, or infant formula resulted in an AUC that was bioequivalent to administration of the intact capsule under fasting conditions.
  Special Populations
    Name
    Hepatic Impairment
    Description
    The pharmacokinetics of efavirenz are not significantly altered in patients with mild hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class A). Pharmacokinetic data are insufficient to recommend use in patients with moderate to severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class B and C).
    Name
    Renal Impairment
    Description
    The pharmacokinetics of efavirenz have not been studied in patient with renal impairment. However, less than 1% of efavirenz is excreted unchanged in the urine, so the impact of renal impairment on efavirenz elimination should be minimal.
    Name
    Pediatrics
    Description
    Infants 3 months and older, Children, and Adolescents
Steady-state pharmacokinetic parameters of efavirenz in pediatric patients receiving the FDA-labeled weight-based dosing via the capsule sprinkle method of administration were predicted by a population pharmacokinetic model to be as follows: AUC (range, 220.52 to 284.28 micromolar x hour); Cmax (range, 5.81 to 7.75 mcg/mL); Cmin (range, 2.3 to 2.87 mcg/mL). These were predicted to be similar to parameters noted in adults. Some data (n = 43) suggest that clearance may be higher in younger patients as compared to older pediatric populations and adults. The half-life in patients less than 3 years (n = 6) was 11.4 hours as compared to a half-life of 17 hours in patients 3 to 12 years (n = 17) and 23.5 hours in patients 13 to 21 years (n = 13).
    Name
    Gender Differences
    Description
    According to the manufacturer, the pharmacokinetics of efavirenz in males and females appear to be similar. However, a multicenter study involving 255 patients found efavirenz plasma concentrations to be significantly higher in the female population than in males (mean 4 mg/L vs. 2.8 mg/L, p < 0.001, 95% CI 0.6 to 1.8). The authors state these gender differences were independent of body composition or hormonal contraceptive use.
    Name
    Ethnic Differences
    Description
    According to the manufacturer, the pharmacokinetics of efavirenz appear to be similar among different racial groups. However, published literature have reported higher plasma concentrations in patients of African and Asian descent, as well as Latino patients, compared to Caucasian patients. Studies evaluating these racial differences attribute the elevated concentrations to single nucleotide polymorphisms (SNP) in CYP2B6, the enzyme primarily responsible for efavirenz metabolism. Patients with the SNP experience reduced CYP2B6 activity resulting in impaired efavirenz metabolism and elevated plasma concentrations. In a study evaluating the pharmacokinetics of efavirenz in 58 healthy subjects with CYP2B6 polymorphisms, the mean Cmax was found to be 2.25-fold higher in subjects with the CYP2B6 *6/*6 genotype than in those with the CYP2B6 *1/*1 genotype.
    Name
    Other
    Description
    Pregnancy
Pregnancy does not significantly alter systemic concentrations (AUC) of efavirenz; however, the efavirenz geographic mean trough concentration (Cmin) has been found to be 24% lower during the third trimester as compared to postpartum (1.6 versus 2.05 mcg/mL; p = 0.01). Despite these lower values, the Cmin for most women (88%) still exceeds the minimum target concentration associated with virologic efficacy (1 mcg/mL). No dosage adjustments are necessary. There is moderate placental transfer to the fetus.
  Text
  Efavirenz is administered orally. Once in systemic circulation, it is highly protein bound (greater than 99%), predominantly to albumin. In HIV-infected patients (n = 9) receiving 200 to 600 mg once daily for at least 1 month, cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) concentrations ranged from 0.26% to 1.19% (mean 0.69%) of the corresponding plasma concentration. This is approximately 3-times higher than the non-protein bound fraction of efavirenz in plasma. Efavirenz is metabolized in the liver by cytochrome P450 (CYP) 3A and CYP2B6 to hydroxylated metabolites with subsequent glucuronidation to inactive metabolites. The terminal half-life is 52 to 76 hours after a single dose and 40 to 55 hours after multiple doses, as the drug induces its own metabolism. A mass balance/excretion study showed approximately 14% to 34% of radiolabeled efavirenz was excreted in urine and 16% to 61% in feces. Nearly all of the urinary excretion was in the form of metabolites, while efavirenz accounted for the majority of the total radioactivity measured in the feces.
 
Affected cytochrome P450 isoenzymes: CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP3A, CYP2B6
Efavirenz has been shown in vivo to induce CYP3A and CYP2B6. In vitro studies have shown it inhibits CYP2C9 and CYP2C19 enzymes with Ki values (8.5 to 17 microM) in the range of observed plasma concentrations. Efavirenz inhibited CYP2D6 and CYP1A2 only at concentrations well above those achieved clinically (Ki values 82 to 160 microM). Coadministration with drugs primarily metabolized by CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP3A, or CYP2B6 may result in altered plasma concentrations of the coadministered drug. Drugs that induce CYP3A or CYP2B6 activity would be expected to increase the clearance of efavirenz.

Administration
  General Information
  For storage information, see the specific product information within the How Supplied section
 
NOTE: Interruptions of therapy may be necessary for specific situations.
If an unanticipated short-term treatment interruption (i.e., less than 1 to 2 days) is necessary, it is generally recommended that all antiretroviral agents be discontinued simultaneously, especially if the interruption occurs in a pregnant patient or is because of serious toxicity.
If a short-term treatment interruption is anticipated, as in the case of elective surgery, consider the pharmacokinetic properties and food requirements of the specific antiretroviral medications. Stopping a treatment regimen containing medications with differing half-lives may result in functional monotherapy of the antiretroviral with the longest half-life and may increase the risk for resistant mutations.
Health care providers are advised to restart a complete and effective antiretroviral regimen as soon as possible after a short-term interruption of therapy.
Planned long-term treatment interruptions are not recommended due to the potential for HIV disease progression (i.e., declining CD4 counts, viral rebound, acute viral syndrome), development of minor HIV-associated manifestations or serious non-AIDS complications, development of drug resistance, increased risk of HIV transmission, and increased risk for opportunistic infections.
If therapy must be discontinued, counsel the patient on the potential risks and closely monitor for any clinical or laboratory abnormalities.
  Route Specific Administrations
    Administration Details
      Name
      Oral Solid Formulations
      Description
      Swallow capsules and tablets intact with liquid; DO NOT crush the tablets.
 
Capsule sprinkle method of administration
Only for patients unable to swallow capsules or tablets.
Open the capsule by holding it horizontally over a small container and carefully twisting it to open. Take precautions to avoid spillage or dispersion of the contents into the air.
For patients able to tolerate solid foods, add a small amount (1 to 2 teaspoons) of age-appropriate soft food (applesauce, grape jelly, yogurt) to the container holding the entire capsule contents; gently mix and administer the dose.
For young infants, the entire capsule content is mixed in a medicine cup with 2 teaspoons (10 mL) of reconstituted, room temperature infant formula. After carefully stirring with a small spoon, the mixture is drawn up into a 10 mL oral syringe and administered.
After administration, an additional small amount (2 teaspoons) of food or formula must be added to the empty mixing container, stirred to disperse remaining drug residue, and administered to the patient.
Administer the dose within 30 minutes of mixing.
No additional food should be consumed for 2 hours after the dose.
The mixture with grape jelly was rated as the most palatable formulation.
    Name
    Oral Administration
    Description
    Administer on an empty stomach. Administration with food results in elevated efavirenz concentrations, which may lead to an increased frequency of adverse reactions.
Bedtime dosing is recommended to reduce nervous system side effects.

Dosage Adjustment Guidelines
  Hepatic Impairment
  No dosage adjustments are needed for patients with mild hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh A). Not recommended for use in patients with moderate or severe impairment (Child-Pugh B or C) as there are insuffiicent data to determine if dosage adjustments are necessary.
  Renal Impairment
  Efavirenz has not been studied in patients with renal impairment; however, since less than 1% is excreted into the urine unchanged, no dosage adjustments are required.
